Quantaloid-enriched categories: Factorization, weak classifiers, and symmetry
Javier Gutiérrez García, Ulrich Höhle

Abstract
. This paper provides a comprehensive overview of some of the foundational properties of categories enriched over quantaloids, along with several new results. We demonstrate that the category whose objects are quantaloid-enriched categories and whose morphisms are left adjoint distributors admits an (epi, extremal mono)–factorization system. Furthermore, we prove that the category of cocomplete quantaloid-enriched categories satisfies the weak subobject classifier axiom, under stability conditions on the underlying quantaloid. As an application, we discuss how these structural results extend to quantale-valued sets, thereby generalizing the classical theory of Ω \Omega -valued sets.



†
†
footnotetext:
MSC2020: Primary 18D20 Secondary 06F07.

Introduction

Quantaloid-enriched categories generalize sheaves on locales (cf.
[
25
]
) and can be understood as temporally dynamic extensions of quantale-enriched categories.
This survey is intended to unify and extend existing results, while offering a coherent framework for further exploration of categorical structures enriched in quantaloids.

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be a small quantaloid. We begin by providing a detailed treatment of
\mathcal{Q}
-enriched presheaves and their role in establishing cocompleteness and constructing the Cauchy completion of
\mathcal{Q}
-enriched categories.

Beyond these foundational aspects, we introduce new structural insights into
\mathcal{Q}
-enriched categories, including:

–

When the underlying quantaloid
\mathcal{Q}
is stable, the category of separated and cocomplete
\mathcal{Q}
-enriched categories satisfies the weak subobject classifier axiom (cf.
[
14
]
).

–

The category
\mathcal{Q}
-
\mathtt{Set}
, consisting of
\mathcal{Q}
-enriched categories and left adjoint distributors, forms an (epi, extremal mono)-category. Our proof relies on a characterization of epimorphisms and extremal monomorphisms in
\mathcal{Q}
-
\mathtt{Set}
, which has only appeared in fragmented form in the literature (cf.
[
18
]
).

–

We provide a complete characterization of the conditions under which the Cauchy completion preserves the symmetry axiom of
\mathcal{Q}
-enriched categories, and we explore several notable examples arising from involutive quantales.

As a concluding perspective, we examine how these constructions manifest
in the context of quantale-valued sets,
which can be interpreted as symmetric, quantale-valued preordered sets.
In this setting, the underlying involutive quantaloids are derived from involutive and unital quantales via diagonal arrows — a construction originally introduced by I. Stubbe (cf.
[
24
]
).

The paper is organized as follows. We begin in Section
1
by exploring general properties of small quantaloids and their relationship to quantales, including those that are not necessarily unital. This sets the stage for the development of the theory of quantaloid-enriched categories. In Section
2
, we introduce the notion of
\mathcal{Q}
-enriched categories and distributors, and establish the foundational properties of adjoint distributors and functors. Section
3
is devoted to
\mathcal{Q}
-enriched presheaves, including covariant and contravariant variants, and the construction of the presingleton space, which plays a central role in later sections. In Section
4
, we explore cocompleteness in the enriched setting, characterize cocontinuous functors, and prove that the category of separated and cocomplete
\mathcal{Q}
-enriched categories admits weak subobject classifiers of specific types. In Section
5
, we study the category
\mathcal{Q}
-Set of
\mathcal{Q}
-enriched categories and left adjoint distributors, and show that it forms an (epi,extremal mono)-category. This includes a detailed analysis of epimorphisms and extremal monomorphisms, culminating in a canonical factorization result. In Section
6
we recall the Cauchy completion and give a full characterization of the property that the Cauchy completion preserves the symmetry axiom. Finally Section
7
serves as an epilogue, illustrating the previous results in the context of quantale-valued sets.

1.
Quantaloids and their relationship to quantales

Let
\mathtt{Sup}
be the category of complete lattices and join-preserving maps, equipped with the tensor product of complete lattices (cf.
[
5
, Sect. 2.1.2]
). It is well known that
\mathtt{Sup}
is a symmetric and monoidal closed category.

A
small quantaloid
is a small category
\mathcal{Q}
with the following additional properties:

\bullet

Each hom-set is a complete lattice.

\bullet

The composition of morphisms
\circ\colon\text{\rm hom}({q},{r})\times\text{\rm hom}({p},{q})\rightarrow\text{\rm hom}({p},{r})
preserves arbitrary joins in both variables.

As a result, quantaloids can be viewed as categories enriched over
\mathtt{Sup}
. Hence for each pair of objects
{p}
and
{q}
of a quantaloid the hom-set
\text{\rm hom}({p},{q})
is a hom-space given by some complete lattice.
Regarding notation, we recall that domain and codomain are intrinsic to the notion of a
morphism
(cf.
[
2
, Sect. 1.2]
). Thus, for a morphism
u\in\text{\rm hom}({p},{q})
, we may also write

{p}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.13771pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.15277pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\textstyle{u}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 15.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 15.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces{q}
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}_{0}
be the set of objects in
\mathcal{Q}
. If two arrows share the same domain (e.g.
{p}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.13771pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.15277pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\textstyle{u}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 15.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 15.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces{q}
and
{p}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.397pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.15277pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\textstyle{v}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 15.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 15.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces{r}
), then

{u}\swarrow{v}:=\mathop{\textstyle\bigvee}\{\,{h}\in\text{\rm hom}({r},{q})\mid{h}\circ{v}\leq{u}\,\}.

If two arrows share the same codomain (e.g.
{p}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.13771pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.15277pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\textstyle{u}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 15.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 15.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces{q}

and
{r}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.397pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.15277pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\textstyle{v}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 15.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 15.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces{q}
), we define:

{v}\searrow{u}:=\mathop{\textstyle\bigvee}\{\,{h}\in\text{\rm hom}({p},{r})\mid{v}\circ{h}\leq{u}\,\}.

Furthermore, for each object
{p}\in\mathcal{Q}_{0}
,
the hom-space
\text{\rm hom}({p},{p})

forms a unital quantale, with the unit element denoted by
1_{p}
.
Conversely, any unital quantale can be regarded as the hom-space of a quantaloid with a single object. In this sense, quantaloids can be interpreted as “varying unital quantales”, where each object has its own associated quantale structure, and morphisms between objects reflect interactions between these structures.

A quantaloid
\mathcal{Q}
is said to be
involutive
if there exists a contravariant functor
j\colon{\mathcal{Q}\rightarrow\mathcal{Q}}
enriched in
\mathtt{Sup}
, which acts as the identity on objects and satisfies the involution condition
j\circ j=\text{\rm id}_{\mathcal{Q}}
. Since
j
is enriched in
\mathtt{Sup}
, its action on the hom-spaces is arbitrary join-preserving.
For concepts not defined here, we refer to
[
5
,
19
,
20
]
.

The following remark describes a canonical extension of a quantaloid
\mathcal{Q}
based on its diagonal arrows of
\mathcal{Q}
(cf.
[
24
]
).

Remark 1.1
.

(1) Let
\mathcal{Q}
be a small quantaloid. Then
\mathcal{Q}
induces a new quantaloid
D\mathcal{Q}
constructed as follows:

–

The objects of
D\mathcal{Q}
are the morphisms of
\mathcal{Q}
.

–

The hom-spaces of
D\mathcal{Q}
are constructed using square diagrams in
\mathcal{Q}
of the form

\textstyle{p}
\textstyle{r}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle{v}\searrow{k}}
\textstyle{p}
\textstyle{s}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\textstyle{k}}
\textstyle{r}
\textstyle{s}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\textstyle{v}}
\textstyle{q}
\textstyle{s}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle{k}\swarrow{u}}
\textstyle{p}
\textstyle{q}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\textstyle{u}}
\textstyle{p}
\textstyle{s}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\textstyle k}

Then
\text{\rm hom}({u},{v})
consists of all diagonal arrows making the diagram commutative, i.e.,

\text{\rm hom}({u},{v})=\{\,{k}\in\text{\rm hom}(\text{\rm dom}({u}),\text{\rm codom}({v}))\mid({k}\swarrow{u})\circ{u}={k}={v}\circ({v}\searrow{k})\,\},

which is a complete sublattice of
\text{\rm hom}(\text{\rm dom}({u}),\text{\rm codom}({v}))
in the sense of
\mathtt{Sup}
.

–

The composition of morphisms
{k}\in\text{\rm hom}({u},{v})
and
{l}\in\text{\rm hom}({v},{h})
is defined by:

{l}\mathbin{\circ_{v}}{k}=({l}\swarrow{v})\circ{v}\circ({v}\searrow{k})={l}\circ({v}\searrow{k})=({l}\swarrow{v})\circ{k}.

This composition
\circ_{v}
is clearly join-preserving in each variable separately — i.e.,

\text{\rm hom}({v},{h})\otimes\text{\rm hom}({u},{v})\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.59909pt\raise 5.12848pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.12848pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\circ_{v}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.79297pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 25.58594pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\text{\rm hom}({u},{h})

is join-preserving, where
\otimes
denotes the tensor product in
\mathtt{Sup}
.
†
†

Due to the universal property of the tensor product, any map
X\times Y\rightarrow Z
that preserves arbitrary joins in each variable separately can be identified with a join preserving map
X\otimes Y\rightarrow Z
.

–

Given a pair of objects
u
and
v
in the category
D\mathcal{Q}
,
u
is a right unit and
v
is a left unit for the hom-space
\text{\rm hom}({u},{v})
. In particular,
\text{\rm hom}({u},{u})
forms a unital quantale w.r.t. the composition operation
\circ_{u}
, and
u
itself is the unit element of
\text{\rm hom}({u},{u})
.

Moreover, there exists an embedding functor from
\mathcal{Q}

into
D\mathcal{Q}
, defined as follows. Objects
{p}\in\mathcal{Q}_{0}
are identified with their respective identity morphisms

1_{p}
. Under this identification, the hom-spaces
\text{\rm hom}({p},{q})
and
\text{\rm hom}(1_{p},1_{q})
coincide, and this correspondence preserves the related compositions.

(2) When extending
a small involutive quantaloid
(\mathcal{Q},j)
to an involutive quantaloid via diagonal arrows, the previous construction must be modified.
First, recall that a morphism
u
of
\mathcal{Q}
is
hermitian
if
\text{\rm dom}(u)=\text{\rm codom}(u)
and
j(u)=u
. In particular, identity morphisms in
\mathcal{Q}
are always hermitian. The set of objects of
D\mathcal{Q}
is then defined as the set of all hermitian morphisms of
\mathcal{Q}
and is consequently a subset of
\text{\rm morph}(\mathcal{Q})
.
The constructions of the composition and identities from (1) remain unchanged.
Since now
u
and
v
are hermitian, the involution
j
on
\mathcal{Q}
extends naturally to
D\mathcal{Q}
by setting
\overline{j}(k):=j(k)
for all
k\in\text{\rm hom}(u,v)
. With these modifications,
(D\mathcal{Q},\overline{j})
becomes an involutive quantaloid, and there exists also an embedding functor
\textstyle{(\mathcal{Q},j)}
\textstyle{(D\mathcal{Q},\overline{j})}
as described in (1).

In this context, we recall that a quantale
(\mathfrak{{Q}},\ast)
is a semigroup in
\mathtt{Sup}
, and an involution
j\colon\mathfrak{{Q}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathfrak{{Q}}
on a quantale satisfies
j\circ j=1_{\mathfrak{{Q}}}
and is an anti-homomorphism — i.e., a join-preserving map making the following diagram commutative:

\textstyle{\mathfrak{{Q}}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{{Q}}\otimes\mathfrak{{Q}}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{\ast}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{{Q}}\otimes\mathfrak{{Q}}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{{Q}}\otimes\mathfrak{{Q}}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{{Q}}\otimes\mathfrak{{Q}}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{\ast}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{{Q}}\otimes\mathfrak{{Q}}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{{Q}}\otimes\mathfrak{{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{j\otimes j}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{{Q}}\otimes\mathfrak{{Q}}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{{Q}}\otimes\mathfrak{{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{c_{\mathfrak{{Q}}\mathfrak{{Q}}}}

Here,
c_{\mathfrak{{Q}}\mathfrak{{Q}}}
is a component of the symmetry in
\mathtt{Sup}
. For simplicity, we write
p^{\prime}
for
j(p)
, where
p\in\mathfrak{{Q}}
. Note also that a unital and involutive quantale is precisely the hom-space of an involutive quantaloid with a single object.

Since any unital quantale (i.e., a monoid in
\mathtt{Sup}
) can be identified with the hom-space of a quantaloid with a single object, the construction in Remark
1.1
(1) shows that any
unital
quantale
(\mathfrak{{Q}},\ast,e)
can be embedded into a quantaloid
D\mathfrak{{Q}}
. Furthermore, every unital quantale can be embedded into a unital and involutive quantale via a unital quantale homomorphism (see
[
8
, Sect. 2]
for details). Therefore, in the next remark, we focus on this situation and present the details of the constructions mentioned in Remark
1.1
(2).

Remark 1.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{{Q}}=(\mathfrak{{Q}},\ast,e,\mbox{}^{\prime})
be a unital and involutive quantale, which we extend to the involutive quantaloid
(D\mathfrak{{Q}},j)
by means of diagonal arrows. Then the details are as follows.

–

The objects of
D\mathfrak{{Q}}
are hermitian elements of
\mathfrak{{Q}}
— i.e.,
D\mathfrak{{Q}}_{0}=\{\,{a}\in\mathfrak{{Q}}\mid{a}^{\prime}={a}\,\}
.

–

For each
{a},{b}\in D\mathfrak{{Q}}_{0}
, the hom-space
\text{\rm hom}({a},{b})
is defined by:

\displaystyle\text{\rm hom}({a},{b})

\displaystyle=\{\,{\lambda}\in\mathfrak{{Q}}\mid\exists{\lambda}_{1},{\lambda}_{2}\in\mathfrak{{Q}}:\,\ {\lambda}={\lambda}_{1}\ast{a}={b}\ast{\lambda}_{2}\,\}

\displaystyle=\{\,{\lambda}\in\mathfrak{{Q}}\mid{\lambda}=({\lambda}\swarrow{a})\ast{a}={b}\ast({b}\searrow{\lambda})\,\}

and forms a complete sublattice of
\mathfrak{{Q}}
in the sense of
\mathtt{Sup}
. In particular, the hom-space associated with the unit of
\mathfrak{{Q}}
coincides with
\mathfrak{{Q}}
— i.e.,
\text{\rm hom}({e},{e})=\mathfrak{{Q}}
. Moreover, the hom-spaces whose domain or codomain is the object
\bot
consist of a single arrow. Hence,
\bot
is the zero object in the underlying ordinary category of
D\mathfrak{{Q}}
.

–

The composition of morphisms
{\lambda}\in\text{\rm hom}({a},{b})
and
{\mu}\in\text{\rm hom}({b},{c})
is defined by:

{\mu}\mathbin{\circ_{b}}{\lambda}=({\mu}\swarrow{b})\ast{b}\ast({b}\searrow{\lambda})={\mu}\ast({b}\searrow{\lambda})=({\mu}\swarrow{b})\ast{\lambda}.

This composition
\circ_{b}
is join-preserving in each variable separately. Moreover, since
for all
{\lambda}\in\text{\rm hom}({b},{b})
we have
{b}\mathbin{\circ_{b}}{\lambda}={\lambda}={\lambda}\mathbin{\circ_{b}}{b}
, it
follows that
{b}
acts as the unit of the quantale
\text{\rm hom}({b},{b})
.
It is also straightforward to verify that the composition coincides with the quantale multiplication if and only if all hermitian elements of
\mathfrak{Q}
are idempotent. In this context the quantale multiplication is read from right to left.

–

The involution
j
on
D\mathfrak{{Q}}
is inherited from the involution
\mbox{}^{\prime}
on
\mathfrak{{Q}}
— i.e.,
j(\lambda)=\lambda^{\prime}
.

If
\mathfrak{{Q}}=(\mathfrak{{Q}},\ast,e)
is a commutative and unital quantale (which is trivially an involutive quantale w.r.t. the identity of
\mathfrak{{Q}}
), then the residuals satisfy:

a\rightarrow b:=a\searrow b=b\swarrow a

for all
{a},{b}\in\mathfrak{{Q}}
. In this case we adopt the notation
\rightarrow
for the residual, and consequently the hom-spaces in
D\mathfrak{{Q}}
take the form:

\text{\rm hom}({a},{b})=\{\,{\lambda}\in\mathfrak{{Q}}\mid{\lambda}={a}\ast({a}\rightarrow{\lambda})={b}\ast({b}\rightarrow{\lambda})\,\}.

Thus,
(D\mathfrak{{Q}},j)
becomes an involutive quantaloid, where the involution is
the bijection

j\colon\text{\rm hom}({a},{b})\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\text{\rm hom}({b},{a}),\qquad j({a}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.08333pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.47223pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\textstyle{\lambda}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 15.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 15.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces{b})={b}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.08333pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.47223pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\textstyle{\lambda}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 15.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 15.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces{a}.

Furthermore, each object
{a}\in\mathfrak{{Q}}
is the unit of the its endomorphism quantale
\text{\rm hom}({a},{a})
, and the composition of
{\lambda}\in\text{\rm hom}({a},{b})
and
{\mu}\in\text{\rm hom}({b},{c})
is given by:

(1.1)

{\mu}\mathbin{\circ_{b}}{\lambda}={b}\ast({b}\rightarrow{\mu})\ast({b}\rightarrow{\lambda})={\mu}\ast({b}\rightarrow{\lambda})={\lambda}\ast({b}\rightarrow{\mu}).

Finally, if
\mathfrak{{Q}}
is commutative and integral (i.e., the unit
e
is the top element of
\mathfrak{{Q}}
) then for all
{\lambda}\in\text{\rm hom}({a},{b})
, the relation
{\lambda}\leq{a}\wedge{b}
holds. In this setting, the top element of
\text{\rm hom}({a},{a})
coincides with
{a}
, making each endomorphism quantale
\text{\rm hom}({a},{a})
integral.

Historical Remark 1.3
.

If
\mathfrak{{Q}}
is an integral and idempotent quantale (i.e., a frame), then the quantaloid
(D\mathfrak{{Q}},j)
corresponds to a construction originally introduced by Walters in 1981 (
[
25
]
).

We now generalize this construction to the setting of involutive quantales, which need not be unital.

Remark 1.4
.

(Cf.
[
12
, Sect. 6]
) Let
\mathfrak{{Q}}=(\mathfrak{{Q}},\ast,\mbox{}^{\prime})
be an involutive quantale. Then
\mathfrak{{Q}}
induces an involutive quantaloid
(D\mathfrak{{Q}},j)
as follows. We begin by fixing some terminology: An element
a\in\mathfrak{{Q}}
is
self-divisible
if it is both a left
and
a right divisor of itself. Clearly, every idempotent element of
\mathfrak{{Q}}
is self-divisible.
With this in place, the construction of the involutive quantaloid
(D\mathfrak{{Q}},j)
proceeds as follows:

–

The objects of
D\mathfrak{{Q}}
are all self-divisible and hermitian elements of
\mathfrak{{Q}}
.

–

For each pair
{a},{b}\in D\mathfrak{{Q}}_{0}
, the hom-space
\text{\rm hom}({a},{b})
is defined as in Remark
1.2
, namely:
†
†

In comparison with
[
12
, Sect. 6]
and
[
11
, Ex. 1.3]
, we note that in this presentation we adopt the convention that, in the case of idempotent quantales, the quantale multiplication is interpreted as composition in the categorical sense. Furthermore, we do not impose the condition that elements of
\text{\rm hom}(a,b)
must be below
a\wedge b
. Instead, we define
\text{\rm hom}(a,b)
as the set of elements
{\lambda}\in\mathfrak{{Q}}
such that
a
is a right divisor of
{\lambda}
and
b
is a left divisor of
{\lambda}
. This approach was presented by H. Lai and D. Zhang at the 3rd International Conference on Quantitative Logic and Soft Computing, Xi’an, China, 2012.

\text{\rm hom}({a},{b})=\{\,{\lambda}\in\mathfrak{{Q}}\mid{\lambda}=({\lambda}\swarrow{a})\ast{a}={b}\ast({b}\searrow{\lambda}\,\}

and is a complete sublattice of
\mathfrak{Q}
in the sense of
\mathtt{Sup}
,
since
\ast
is join-preserving in each variable separately. Again the universal lower bound in
\mathfrak{{Q}}
is the zero object in the underlying ordinary category of
D\mathfrak{{Q}}
.

The definitions of
composition
and
involution
remain as in Remark
1.2
, including the treatment of commutative quantales.
In particular, since all objects in
D\mathfrak{{Q}}
are
self-divisible
, each object
{a}
again serves as the unit of the quantale
\text{\rm hom}({a},{a})
. If all self-divisible and hermitian elements of
\mathfrak{Q}
are idempotent, then again the composition coincides with the quantale multiplication.

This previous construction extends that of Remark
1.2
: if the involutive quantale
\mathfrak{{Q}}=(\mathfrak{{Q}},\ast,\mbox{}^{\prime})
is unital, then every element is self-divisible, and the two constructions of
(D\mathfrak{{Q}},j)
coincide.
However, in the non-unital case, some elements of
\mathfrak{{Q}}
may not be self-divisible. As a result, the involutive quantaloid
(D\mathfrak{{Q}},j)
may also arise from a unital subquantale of
\mathfrak{{Q}}
, as illustrated in the next example.

Example 1.5
.

Consider an involutive quantale
\mathfrak{{Q}}
with at least
3
elements, satisfying the condition:

a\ast b=\top,\qquad\text{for all }a,b\in\mathfrak{{Q}}\setminus\{\,\bot\,\}.

Under this assumption, every element of
\mathfrak{{Q}}\setminus\{\,\bot,\top\,\}
fails to be self-divisible. In particular,
\mathfrak{{Q}}
is not unital and the set of objects in the associated quantaloid reduces to
D\mathfrak{{Q}}_{0}=\{\,\bot,\top\,\}
. Consequently, the involutive quantaloid
(D\mathfrak{{Q}},j)
is isomorphic to

(D\boldsymbol{2},j)
, where
\boldsymbol{2}
denotes the
unital
and commutative quantale on the
2
-chain
\{\,\bot,\top\,\}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\top}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\top}

However, in general, the construction described in Remark
1.4
yields quantaloids that cannot be derived from a unital quantale.
We present three examples illustrating this situation.

.

be a commutative quantale defined on the lattice

–

. Indeed, since

can serve as a unit in a quantale with at least 4 elements.

and its quantization
\mathfrak{Q}_{\boldsymbol{2}}
.

We conclude this section with a property of quantaloids that plays an important role in Section
4
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be a quantaloid and let
{p}\in\mathcal{Q}_{0}
. Then
\mathcal{Q}
is said to be
p
-stable
, if the following condition holds for all
{q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{0}
:

1_{q}\leq(\mathop{\textstyle\bigvee}\text{\rm hom}(p,q))\circ(\mathop{\textstyle\bigvee}\text{\rm hom}(q,p)).

A quantaloid
\mathcal{Q}
is
stable
if there exists an object
p\in\mathcal{Q}_{0}
such that
\mathcal{Q}
is
p
-stable.

Remarks 1.9
.

(1) If
\mathfrak{{Q}}
is an involutive quantale and
{a}\in D\mathfrak{{Q}}_{0}
, then
(D\mathfrak{{Q}},j)
is
a
-stable if and only if
{b}\leq(\mathop{\textstyle\bigvee}\text{\rm hom}({a},{b}))\mathbin{\circ_{{a}}}(\mathop{\textstyle\bigvee}\text{\rm hom}({b},{a}))
for all
{b}\in D\mathfrak{{Q}}_{0}
. Moreover, if

is

\mathfrak{{Q}}
is idempotent — i.e.,
\mathfrak{{Q}}
is a frame (cf. Historical Remark
1.3
).

(2) If
\mathfrak{{Q}}
is an involutive and integral quantale with a non-idempotent and
hermitian element
b
,
then
(D\mathfrak{{Q}},j)
is
not

\top
-stable, since
(\mathop{\textstyle\bigvee}\text{\rm hom}(\top,b))\mathbin{\circ_{\top}}(\mathop{\textstyle\bigvee}\text{\rm hom}(b,\top))=b\ast b<b
.

(3) Regarding Examples
1.6
,
1.7
and
1.8
, we observe the following:
All involutive quantaloids discussed are stable. More precisely, each one is
p
-stable for all
p\in D\mathfrak{{Q}}_{0}\setminus\{\bot\}
.

2.
\mathcal{Q}
-enriched categories

A
typed set
is an object
X
of the slice category
\mathtt{Set}/\mathcal{Q}_{0}
— i.e., a map
t\colon X_{0}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{Q}_{0}
. Since we treat
t
as a generic notation, we will also denote it by
|{\phantom{x}}|
, interpreting

|{x}|
as the type of the element
x\in X_{0}
.
A
\mathcal{Q}
-enriched category
(or
\mathcal{Q}
-category, for short) is a pair
(X,\alpha)
where
X=(X_{0},t)
is a typed set, and
\alpha\colon X_{0}\times X_{0}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\text{\rm morph}(\mathcal{Q})
is a hom-arrow-assignment satisfying the following conditions:

(C1)

\alpha(x,y)\in\text{\rm hom}(|y|,|x|),\qquad x,y\in X_{0}
.

(C2)

\alpha(x,y)\circ\alpha(y,z)\leq\alpha(x,z),\qquad x,y,z\in X_{0}.

(C3)

1_{|x|}\leq\alpha(x,x),\qquad x\in X_{0}
.

As an immediate consequence of
(C2)
and
(C3)
we obtain the following identity:

(2.1)

\alpha(x,x)\circ\alpha(x,y)=\alpha(x,y)=\alpha(x,y)\circ\alpha(y,y),\qquad x,y\in X_{0}.

In particular,
\alpha(x,x)
is idempotent w.r.t. the composition for all
x\in X_{0}
.

Comment 2.1
.

Let
{p}
be an object of a quantaloid
\mathcal{Q}
, and let
(X,\alpha)
be a
\mathcal{Q}
-category. Define the
{p}
-section
of
X
as
X_{p}=\{\,x\in X_{0}\mid|x|={p}\,\}
.
Then the restriction of
\alpha
to
X_{p}\times X_{p}
yields a quantale-enriched category. This observation allows us to interpret quantaloid-enriched categories as
time-varying
quantale-enriched categories, where the enrichment varies over the objects of the quantaloid.

Example 2.2
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be a quantaloid, which we now interpret as a typed set — i.e., we consider the identity map
1_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}
on the set of objects of
\mathcal{Q}
as the type map, and we continue to denote the pair
(\mathcal{Q}_{0},1_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}})
simply by
\mathcal{Q}
, provided no confusion arises. We define a hom-arrow-assignment
\tau\colon\mathcal{Q}_{0}\times\mathcal{Q}_{0}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\text{\rm morph}(\mathcal{Q})
by
\tau({p},{q})=\mathop{\textstyle\bigvee}\text{\rm hom}({q},{p})
for each
{p},{q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{0}
.
Then the pair
(\mathcal{Q},\tau)
forms a
\mathcal{Q}
-category.

Let
(X,\alpha)
and
(Y,\beta)
be
\mathcal{Q}
-categories. A
distributor

\Phi\colon(X,\alpha)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.06456pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.62848pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\circ}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 13.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 13.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces(Y,\beta)
is a map
\Phi\colon Y_{0}\times X_{0}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\text{\rm morph}(\mathcal{Q})
satisfying the following conditions for all
x,x_{1},x_{2}\in X_{0}
and
y,y_{1},y_{2}\in Y_{0}
:

(D1)

\Phi(y,x)\in\text{\rm hom}(|x|,|y|)
.

(D2)

\beta(y_{1},y_{2})\circ\Phi(y_{2},x)\leq\Phi(y_{1},x)
and
\Phi(y,x_{1})\circ\alpha(x_{1},x_{2})\leq\Phi(y,x_{2})
.

With regard to
(C3)
and
(D2)
every distributor
\Phi\colon(X,\alpha)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.06456pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.62848pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\circ}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 13.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 13.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces(Y,\beta)
satisfies the following:

(2.2)

\Phi(y,x)=\beta(y,y)\circ\Phi(y,x)=\Phi(y,x)\circ\alpha(x,x),\qquad x\in X_{0},\,y\in Y_{0}.

The composition of distributors
\Phi\colon(X,\alpha)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.06456pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.62848pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\circ}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 13.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 13.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces(Y,\beta)
and
\Psi\colon(Y,\beta)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.06456pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.62848pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\circ}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 13.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 13.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces(Z,\gamma)
is defined by:

(\Psi\otimes\Phi)(z,x)=\mathop{\textstyle\bigvee}_{y\in Y_{0}}\Psi(z,y)\circ\Phi(y,x),\qquad(z,x)\in Z_{0}\times X_{0}.

The hom-arrow-assignment
\alpha\colon(X,\alpha)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.06456pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.62848pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\circ}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 13.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 13.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces(X,\alpha)
is itself a distributor and serves as the identity w.r.t. the composition
\otimes
. Since the pointwise join of distributors is again a distributor, the collection of
\mathcal{Q}
-categories with distributors as morphisms forms a quantaloid, and in particular, a
2
-category.

Two distributors
\Phi\colon(X,\alpha)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.06456pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.62848pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\circ}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 13.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 13.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces(Y,\beta)
and
\Psi\colon(Y,\beta)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.06456pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.62848pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\circ}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 13.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 13.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces(X,\alpha)
are adjoint if and only if the following relations hold for all
x_{1},x_{2}\in X_{0}
and
y_{1},y_{2}\in Y_{0}
:

\alpha(x_{1},x_{2})\leq(\Psi\otimes\Phi)(x_{1},x_{2})\quad\text{and}\quad(\Phi\otimes\Psi)(y_{1},y_{2})\leq\beta(y_{1},y_{2}).

In light of
(D2)
,
2.1
and
2.2
, adjointness of distributors is equivalent to the following conditions for all
x\in X_{0}
and
y_{1},y_{2}\in Y
:

(D3)

1_{|x|}\leq\mathop{\textstyle\bigvee}_{y\in Y_{0}}\Psi(x,y)\circ\Phi(y,x)\quad\text{and}\quad\Phi(y_{1},x)\circ\Psi(x,y_{2})\leq\beta(y_{1},y_{2})
.

We denote this adjointness by
\Phi\dashv\Psi
, where
\Phi
is the left adjoint distributor and
\Psi
the right adjoint distributor. Since the right adjoint distributor
\Psi
is uniquely determined by the left adjoint
\Phi
,
\Psi
can be expressed by
\Phi
as follows:

(2.3)

\Psi(x,y)=\mathop{\textstyle\bigvee}_{y_{1}\in Y_{0}}\Phi(y_{1},x)\searrow\beta(y_{1},y),\qquad x\in X_{0},\,y\in Y.

Let
(X,\alpha)
and
(Y,\beta)
be
\mathcal{Q}
-categories.
A
functor

\varphi\colon(X,\alpha)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>(Y,\beta)
is a morphism in the slice category
\mathtt{Set}/\mathcal{Q}_{0}
— i.e., a function
\varphi\colon X_{0}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Y_{0}
making the following diagram commute

\textstyle{X_{0}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{|\phantom{x}|}
\textstyle{X_{0}}
\textstyle{Y_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{Y_{0}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{|\phantom{x}|}

and satisfying the following enrichment condition, which has its origin in
[
4
]
:

(m)

\alpha(x_{1},x_{2})\leq\beta(\varphi(x_{1}),\varphi(x_{2})),\qquad x_{1},x_{2}\in X_{0}
.

Since the axioms
(C2)
and
(C3)
are preserved under pointwisely defined meets of hom-arrow-assignments, the category
\mathtt{Cat}(\mathcal{Q})
of
\mathcal{Q}
-categories and functors is topological over
\mathtt{Set}/\mathcal{Q}_{0}
w.r.t. the forgetful functor from
\mathtt{Cat}(\mathcal{Q})
to
\mathtt{Set}/\mathcal{Q}_{0}
. In particular, for any type map
|\phantom{x}|\colon X_{0}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{Q}_{0}
the
discrete

\mathcal{Q}
-category
(X,\delta)
is given by:

\delta(x,y)=\begin{cases}\bot,&\text{if }x\neq y,\\
1_{|x|},&\text{if }x=y,\end{cases}\qquad x,y\in X_{0}.

If
\varphi\colon(X,\alpha)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>(Y,\beta)
is a functor, it induces a pair of adjoint distributors
\varphi_{b}\dashv\varphi^{b}
between
(X,\alpha)
and
(Y,\beta)
defined by:

\varphi_{b}(y,x)=\beta(y,\varphi(x))\quad\text{and}\quad\varphi^{b}(x,y)=\beta(\varphi(x),y),\qquad x\in X_{0},\,y\in Y_{0}.

Obviously this correspondence describes a special relationship between functors and left adjoint distributors. Anticipating the terminology at the beginning of Section
4
this relationship is injective, if the codomain of
\varphi
is separated.

Let
(X,\alpha)
be a
\mathcal{Q}
-category. For each element
x\in X_{0}
we can associate a singleton
\mathcal{Q}
-category
(\{x\},\gamma)
, where we restrict the type function to
\{x\}
and

\gamma(x,x)=\alpha(x,x)
.
If
\Phi\colon(X,\alpha)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.06456pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.62848pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\circ}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 13.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 13.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces(Y,\beta)

is left adjoint to thedistributor
\Psi\colon(Y,\beta)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.06456pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.62848pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\circ}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 13.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 13.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces(X,\alpha)
, then for each
x\in X_{0}
it induces a left adjoint distributor
\tau_{x}\colon(\{x\},\gamma)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.06456pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.62848pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\circ}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 13.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 13.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces(Y,\beta)
defined by

\tau_{x}(y,x)=\Phi(y,x),\qquad y\in Y_{0}.

The corresponding right adjoint distributor
\sigma_{x}\colon(Y,\beta)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.06456pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.62848pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\circ}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 13.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 13.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces(\{x\},\gamma)
is determined by

\sigma_{x}(x,y)=\Psi(x,y),\qquad y\in Y_{0}.

Moreover,
\Phi
is left adjoint if and only if
\tau_{x}
is left adjoint for all
x\in X_{0}
.

Proposition 2.3
.

Let
{\Phi,\Phi^{\prime}\colon(X,\alpha)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.06456pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.62848pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\circ}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 13.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 13.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces(Y,\beta)}
and
\Psi,\Psi^{\prime}\colon(Y,\beta)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.06456pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.62848pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\circ}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 13.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 13.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces(X,\alpha)
be pairs of distributors such that
\Phi\dashv\Psi
,
\Phi^{\prime}\dashv\Psi^{\prime}
,
\Phi^{\prime}\leq\Phi
and
\Psi^{\prime}\leq\Psi
.
Then
\Phi=\Phi^{\prime}
and
\Psi=\Psi^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Using (
2.2
) and the axioms
(D2)
and
(D3)
we compute:

\displaystyle\Phi(y,x)

\displaystyle=\Phi(y,x)\circ\alpha(x,x)\leq\mathop{\textstyle\bigvee}_{z\in Y}\Phi(y,x)\circ\Psi^{\prime}(x,z)\circ\Phi^{\prime}(z,x)

\displaystyle\leq\mathop{\textstyle\bigvee}_{z\in Y}\Phi(y,x)\circ\Psi(x,z)\circ\Phi^{\prime}(z,x)\leq\mathop{\textstyle\bigvee}_{z\in Y}\beta(y,z)\circ\Phi^{\prime}(z,x)=\Phi^{\prime}(y,x).

Hence
\Phi=\Phi^{\prime}
, and by adjointness, it follows that
\Psi=\Psi^{\prime}
.
∎

Remark 2.4

(Terminal object in
\mathtt{Cat}(\mathcal{Q})
)
.

Recall from Example
2.2
that the identity map
1_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}
serves as the type map for the
\mathcal{Q}
-category
(\mathcal{Q},\tau)
. For any
\mathcal{Q}
-category
(X,\alpha)
, the type map
|{\phantom{x}}|\colon X_{0}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{Q}_{0}
is the unique functor
|{\phantom{x}}|\colon(X,\alpha)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>(\mathcal{Q},\tau)
.
Therefore,
(\mathcal{Q},\tau)
is the terminal object in the category
\mathtt{Cat}(\mathcal{Q})
.

3.
\mathcal{Q}
-enriched presheaves

Let
{p}\in\mathcal{Q}_{0}
be an object of a quantaloid
\mathcal{Q}
. We consider the discrete
\mathcal{Q}
-category
(\{\cdot\},\delta)
on the singleton
\{\cdot\}
with type
p
, where
\delta(\cdot,\cdot)=1_{p}
.
A distributor
\Pi\colon(X,\alpha)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.06456pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.62848pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\circ}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 13.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 13.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces(\{\cdot\},\delta)
is called a
covariant
\mathcal{Q}
-presheaf of type
p
on
(X,\alpha)
. Such a distributor can be identified with a map
{f}\colon X\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\text{\rm morph}(\mathcal{Q})
satisfying the following conditions:

(Q0)

{f}(x)\in\text{\rm hom}(|x|,{p}),\qquad x\in X_{0}
.

(Q1)

{f}(x_{1})\circ\alpha(x_{1},x_{2})\leq{f}(x_{2}),\qquad x_{1},x_{2}\in X_{0}
.

Since
{p}
is the type of
{f}
, we denote a covariant
\mathcal{Q}
-presheaf by the pair
({p},{f})
, where
{p}
is an object of
\mathcal{Q}
and
{f}
is a arrow-valued map satisfying
(Q0)
and
(Q1)
.

Remark 3.1
.

Let
{s}\in\mathcal{Q}_{0}
and define the set

\mathcal{D}_{0}^{s}=\{\,{u}\in\text{\rm morph}(\mathcal{Q})\mid\text{\rm codom}({u})={s}\,\}
.
On
\mathcal{D}_{0}^{s}
the type map
|\phantom{x}|
is given by the restriction of the domain map
dom
to
\mathcal{D}_{0}^{s}
, so the pair
(\mathcal{D}_{0}^{s},|\phantom{x}|)
is denoted by
\mathcal{D}^{s}
.
We define a hom-arrow-assignment
\varkappa\colon\mathcal{D}_{0}^{s}\times\mathcal{D}_{0}^{s}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\text{\rm morph}(\mathcal{Q})
as follows:

\varkappa({p},{q})={p}\searrow{q},\qquad{p},{q}\in\mathcal{D}_{0}^{s}.

Then, a map
{f}\colon X_{0}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{D}_{0}^{s}
is a covariant
\mathcal{Q}
-presheaf of type
{s}
on
(X,\alpha)
if and only if the diagram

\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{|\phantom{x}|}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}_{0}^{s}}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}_{0}^{s}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}
dom

is commutative (cf.
(Q0)
) and the following relation holds (cf.
(Q1)
):

\alpha(x_{2},x_{1})\leq\varkappa({f}(x_{2}),{f}(x_{1})),\qquad x_{1},x_{2}\in X_{0},

— i.e.,
{f}\colon(X,\alpha)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>(\mathcal{D}^{s},\varkappa)
is a functor in
\mathtt{Cat}(\mathcal{Q})
.

A distributor
\Pi\colon(\{\cdot\},\delta)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.06456pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.62848pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\circ}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 13.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 13.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces(X,\alpha)
is called a
contravariant
\mathcal{Q}
-presheaf of type

on
(X,\alpha)
. Such a distributor can be identified with a map
{g}\colon X\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\text{\rm morph}(\mathcal{Q})
satisfying the following conditions:

(P0)

{g}(x)\in\text{\rm hom}({p},|x|),\qquad x\in X_{0}
.

Since

is the type of

, we denote a contravariant
\mathcal{Q}
-presheaf by the pair

where

is an object of
\mathcal{Q}
and

is an arrow-valued map satisfying
(P0)
and
(P1)
.

Remark 3.2
.

Let
{r}\in\mathcal{Q}_{0}
and define the set

\mathcal{R}_{0}^{r}=\{\,{u}\in\text{\rm morph}(\mathcal{Q})\mid\text{\rm dom}({u})={r}\,\}
.
On
\mathcal{R}_{0}^{r}
the type map
|\phantom{x}|
is given by the restriction of the codomain map
codom
to
\mathcal{R}_{0}^{s}
, so the pair
(\mathcal{R}_{0}^{r},|\phantom{x}|)
is denoted by
\mathcal{R}^{r}
.

We define a hom-arrow-assignment
\varrho\colon\mathcal{R}_{0}^{r}\times\mathcal{R}_{0}^{r}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\text{\rm morph}(\mathcal{Q})
by:

\varrho({u},{v})={u}\swarrow{v},\qquad{u},{v}\in\mathcal{R}_{0}^{r}.

Then, a map
{g}\colon X_{0}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{R}_{0}^{r}
is a contravariant
\mathcal{Q}
-presheaf of type
{r}
on
(X,\alpha)
if and only if the diagram

\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{|\phantom{x}|}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{\mathcal{R}_{0}^{r}}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{\mathcal{R}_{0}^{r}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}
codom

is commutative (cf.
(P0)
) and the following relation holds (cf.
(P1)
):

\alpha(x_{1},x_{2})\leq\varrho({g}(x_{1}),{g}(x_{2})),\qquad x_{1},x_{2}\in X_{0},

— i.e.,
{g}\colon(X,\alpha)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>(\mathcal{R}^{r},\varrho)
is a functor in
\mathtt{Cat}(\mathcal{Q})
.

Remark 3.3
.

Let
(\mathcal{Q},j)
be an involutive quantaloid. Then the
dual
\mathcal{Q}
-category

(X,\alpha^{op})
of
(X,\alpha)
exists — i.e.,

\alpha^{op}(x,y)=j(\alpha(y,x)),\qquad x,y\in X_{0}.

Hence, a pair
({p},{g})
is a contravariant
\mathcal{Q}
-presheaf on
(X,\alpha)
if and only if the composition
j\circ{g}\colon(X,\alpha^{op})\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>(D^{p},\varkappa)
is a functor. This condition provides a justification for the use of the term
contravariant
in the chosen terminology.

We now proceed with the construction of
\mathcal{Q}
-categories consisting of covariant (resp. contravariant)
\mathcal{Q}
-presheaves on a given
\mathcal{Q}
-category
(X,\alpha)
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}(X,\alpha)_{0}
be the set of all covariant
\mathcal{Q}
-presheaves on
(X,\alpha)
.
Define the type map
|\phantom{x}|\colon\mathcal{Q}(X,\alpha)_{0}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{Q}_{0}
by
|({p},{f})|={p}
.
Consequently the pair
(\mathcal{Q}(X,\alpha)_{0},|\phantom{x}|)
is denoted by
\mathcal{Q}(X,\alpha)
. It is not difficult to show that the pair
(\mathcal{Q}(X,\alpha),\upsilon)
equipped
with the hom-arrow-assignment
\upsilon
defined by:

\displaystyle\upsilon(({p}_{1},{f}_{1}),({p}_{2},{f}_{2}))

\displaystyle=\mathop{\textstyle\bigwedge}_{x\in X_{0}}({f}_{1}(x)\swarrow{f}_{2}(x))

\displaystyle=\mathop{\textstyle\bigvee}\{{r}\in\text{\rm hom}({p}_{2},{p}_{1})\mid{r}\circ{f}_{2}(x)\leq{f}_{1}(x)\text{ for all }x\in X_{0}\}

forms a
\mathcal{Q}
-category. Finally, if
(\{p\},\delta)
is the discrete
\mathcal{Q}
-category with
|p|=p
, then it is interesting to observe that the
\mathcal{Q}
-categories
(\mathcal{Q}(\{p\},\delta),\upsilon)
and
(\mathcal{R}^{p},\varrho)
are isomorphic.

Similarly, let
P(X,\alpha)_{0}
be the set of all contravariant
\mathcal{Q}
-presheaves on
(X,\alpha)
. Define the type map
|\phantom{x}|\colon P(X,\alpha)_{0}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{Q}_{0}
by
|({p},{g})|={p}
. Consequently the pair
(P(X,\alpha)_{0},|\phantom{x}|)
is denoted by
P(X,\alpha)
.
It is not difficult to show that the pair
(P(X,\alpha),\pi)
equipped with the hom-arrow-assignment
\pi
defined by:

\displaystyle\pi(({p}_{1},{g}_{1}),({p}_{2},{g}_{2}))

\displaystyle=\mathop{\textstyle\bigwedge}_{x\in X_{0}}({g}_{1}(x)\searrow{g}_{2}(x))

\displaystyle=\mathop{\textstyle\bigvee}\{\,{r}\in\text{\rm hom}({p}_{2},{p}_{1})\mid{g}_{1}(x)\circ{r}\leq{g}_{2}(x)\text{ for all }x\in X_{0}\,\}.

forms a
\mathcal{Q}
-category. Again, if
(\{p\},\delta)
is the discrete
\mathcal{Q}
-category with
|p|=p
, then the
\mathcal{Q}
-categories
(P(\{p\},\delta),\pi)
and
(\mathcal{D}^{p},\varkappa)
are isomorphic.

Let
({p},{f})
be a covariant
\mathcal{Q}
-presheaf on
(X,\alpha)
and let
({p},{g})
be a contravariant
\mathcal{Q}
-presheaf on
(X,\alpha)
. If the relation
({p},{g})\dashv({p},{f})
holds — i.e., if
({p},{g})
is a left
adjoint contravariant
\mathcal{Q}
-presheaf, then the triple
\mu=({f},{p},{g})
is called a
presingleton
of the
\mathcal{Q}
-
category
(X,\alpha)
. Referring to (
2.3
) the following relation holds:

(3.1)

{f}(y)=\mathop{\textstyle\bigwedge}_{x\in X_{0}}({g}(x)\searrow\alpha(x,y)),\qquad y\in X_{0}.

Moreover, the type of a presingleton
({f},{p},{g})
is given by
{p}\in\mathcal{Q}_{0}
. This means that if
\widehat{X}_{0}
is the set of all presingletons of the
\mathcal{Q}
-category
(X,\alpha)
,
then the type map
|\phantom{x}|\colon\widehat{X}_{0}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{Q}_{0}
is given by
|({f},{p},{g})|={p}
and allows us to define the pair
(\widehat{X}_{0},|\phantom{x}|)
, which is denoted by
\widehat{X}
.

Since the adjunction
({p},{g})\dashv({p},{f})
is equivalent to the following two conditions:

(P2)

1_{p}\leq\mathop{\textstyle\bigvee}_{x\in X_{0}}({f}(x)\circ{g}(x))
,

(P3)

{g}(x_{1})\circ{f}(x_{2})\leq\alpha(x_{1},x_{2}),\qquad x_{1},x_{2}\in X_{0}
,

there exists an hom-arrow-assignment
\widehat{\alpha}\colon\widehat{X}_{0}\times\widehat{X}_{0}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\text{\rm morph}(\mathcal{Q})
defined by:

(3.2)

\widehat{\alpha}(({f}_{1},{p}_{1},{g}_{1}),({f}_{2},{p}_{2},{g}_{2}))=\mathop{\textstyle\bigvee}_{x\in X_{0}}{f}_{1}(x)\circ{g}_{2}(x).

Thus
(\widehat{X},\widehat{\alpha})
is a
\mathcal{Q}
-category and is called the
presingleton space
of
(X,\alpha)
.

Furthermore, there exists a distributor
\Xi\colon(X,\alpha)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.06456pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.62848pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\circ}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 13.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 13.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces(\widehat{X},\widehat{\alpha})
defined by:

(3.3)

\Xi(\mu,x)={f}(x),\qquad\mu\in\widehat{X}_{0},\,x\in X_{0}.

This distributor is an isomorphism between both
\mathcal{Q}
-categories
(X,\alpha)
and
(\widehat{X},\widehat{\alpha})
in the sense of
\mathcal{Q}
-
\mathtt{Set}
(see Section
5
infra).

Example 3.4
.

Let
(X,\alpha)
be a
\mathcal{Q}
-category.
Then each
x\in X_{0}
determines a presingleton
\widetilde{x}=({f}_{x},|x|,{g}_{x})
,
where
{f}_{x}(y)=\alpha(x,y)
and
{g}_{x}(y)=\alpha(y,x)
for all
y\in X_{0}
.
It follows immediately from the axiom
(Q1)
,
(P1)
,
(C2)
and
(C3)
that the following relations hold for each
x,x_{1},x_{2}\in X_{0}
and
\mu=({f},{p},{g})\in\widehat{X}_{0}
:

(3.4)

\widehat{\alpha}(\widetilde{x},\mu)={g}(x),\quad\widehat{\alpha}(\mu,\widetilde{x})={f}(x)\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{\alpha}(\widetilde{x_{1}},\widetilde{x_{2}})=\alpha(x_{1},x_{2}).

4.
Cocomplete
\mathcal{Q}
-enriched categories

A first application of
\mathcal{Q}
-presheaves is the concept of cocompleteness (resp. completeness) of
\mathcal{Q}
-categories. As a preparation for the separation axiom and the cocompleteness, we introduce the following lemmas.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
(X,\alpha)
be a
\mathcal{Q}
-category and
x,y\in X_{0}
.

(a)

The following properties are equivalent
:

(i)

\alpha(x,x)=\alpha(x,y)
and
\alpha(y,y)=\alpha(y,x)
.

(ii)

1_{|x|}\leq\alpha(x,y)
and
1_{|y|}\leq\alpha(y,x)
.

(b)

The following properties are equivalent
:

(iii)

\alpha(x,x)=\alpha(y,x)
and
\alpha(y,y)=\alpha(x,y)
.

(iv)

1_{|x|}\leq\alpha(y,x)
and
1_{|y|}\leq\alpha(x,y)
.

(c)

If
|x|=|y|
, then the assertion
(i)
is equivalent to
(iii)

Proof.

From axiom
(C3)
, it follows that (i) implies (ii).
Conversely, assume that (ii) holds. Then, using (
2.1
) and
(C2)
, we have:

\alpha(x,x)\leq\alpha(x,x)\circ\alpha(x,y)=\alpha(x,y)\leq\alpha(x,y)\circ\alpha(y,x)\leq\alpha(x,x),

which implies
\alpha(x,x)=\alpha(x,y)
. Similarly, we obtain
\alpha(y,y)=\alpha(y,x)
.

The equivalence in (b) follows analogously to (a), and (c) is a direct corollary of (a) and (b) under the assumption
|x|=|y|
.
∎

A
\mathcal{Q}
-category
(X,\alpha)
is said to be
separated
(or
skeletal
) if the following implication holds for all
x,y\in X_{0}
:

\bigl(|x|=|y|,\quad\alpha(x,x)=\alpha(x,y)\quad\text{and}\quad\alpha(y,y)=\alpha(y,x)\bigr)\implies x=y.

Referring to Lemma
4.1
, this condition is equivalent to the implication (cf. Example
3.4
):

\widetilde{x}=\widetilde{y}\implies x=y.
