7.53885pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\textstyle{(\lambda\ast(|a|\searrow\mu))}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 79.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 79.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces{b}={c}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 7.53885pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\textstyle{((\lambda\swarrow|a|)\ast\mu)}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 79.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 79.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces{b}

for each morphism
{a}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.08333pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.47223pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\textstyle{\lambda}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 15.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 15.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces{b}
and
{c}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.98726pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.18056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\textstyle{\mu}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 15.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 15.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces{a}
of
D\mathfrak{Q}
, we conclude from (
2.1
) that
\psi
satisfies the following property for all
x,y\in X_{0}
:

(7.1)

\psi(x,x)\ast(|x|\searrow\psi(x,y))=\psi(x,y)=(\psi(x,y)\swarrow|y|)\ast\psi(y,y).

Hence
\psi(x,x)
is left divisor of
\psi(x,y)
and
\psi(y,y)
is right divisor of
\psi(x,y)
, and so the divisibility property

(7.2)

\psi(x,x)\ast(\psi(x,x)\searrow\psi(x,y))=\psi(x,y)=(\psi(x,y)\swarrow\psi(y,y))\ast\psi(y,y)

holds for all
x,y\in X_{0}
.
Combining (
7.1
) and (
7.2
), we obtain:

\displaystyle\psi(x,{y})\ast(\psi({y},{y})\searrow\psi({y},{z}))

\displaystyle=(\psi({x},{y})\swarrow|{y}|)\ast\psi({y},{y})\ast(\psi({y},{y})\searrow\psi({y},{z}))

\displaystyle=(\psi({x},{y})\swarrow|{y}|)\ast\psi({y},{z}),

and hence, by axiom
(C2)
the following relation follows:

(7.3)

\psi({x},{y})\ast(\psi({y},{y})\searrow\psi({y},{z}))\leq\psi({x},{z}),\qquad{x},{y},{z}\in X_{0}

A pair
(X_{0},\psi)
is called a
\mathfrak{Q}
-valued preordered set
if
X_{0}
is a set and
\psi\colon X_{0}\times X_{0}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathfrak{Q}
is a map satisfying the conditions (
7.2
) and (
7.3
), referred to as the
\mathfrak{Q}
-preorder
of
(X_{0},\psi)
.

We have shown that every
D\mathfrak{Q}
-category can be understood as a
\mathfrak{Q}
-valued preordered set. This correspondence is a motivation to interpret condition (
7.2
) in terms of preorder semantics.
For this purpose we assume that every element of the underlying quantale
\mathfrak{Q}
is
self-divisible
. Then it is well known that
\mathfrak{Q}
induces two natural preorders on its elements:

–

The left-preorder: For
{a},{b}\in\mathfrak{Q}
, we say that
{b}
is smaller than
{a}
if
{a}
is a left divisor of
{b}
— i.e.,
{b}={a}\ast({a}\searrow{b})
.

–

The right-preorder: Similarly,
{b}
is smaller than
{a}
if
{a}
is right divisor of
{b}
— i.e.,
{b}=({b}\swarrow{a})\ast{a}
.

Given a
\mathfrak{Q}
-preorder
\psi
, we can now interpret its values semantically:
The value
\psi({x},{x})
expresses the
extent
to which
{x}

exists
, while
\psi({x},{y})
expresses the
extent
to which
{x}
is smaller then
{y}
.
Under this interpretation, condition (
7.2
) express the following principle:

The extent to which
{x}
is smaller than
{y}
is bounded by the extent of existence of
{x}
(via the left-preorder) and of
{y}
(via the right-preorder).

This is in line with the foundational idea in logic and ontology that properties of elements require first their existence.

Condition (
7.3
) represents the
transitivity axiom
for
\mathfrak{Q}
-preorders, which differs from traditional approaches in many-valued logics, but is consistent with the
composition law
in the quantaloid
D\mathfrak{Q}
. It ensures that the structure respects the enriched categorical framework while preserving the semantic intuition of orderings.

If
\mathfrak{Q}
is a commutative, unital, and divisible quantale, then the left- and right-preorder coincides with the order of the underlying lattice of
\mathfrak{Q}
. In this case, condition (
7.2
) is equivalent to the strictness condition:

(7.4)

\psi({x},{y})\leq\psi({x},{x})\wedge\psi({y},{y}),\qquad{x},{y}\in X_{0}.

This condition reflects the idea of geometric logic that the extent to which
x
is related to
y
is bounded by the extent to which
x

and

y
exist. It originates from the strictness axioms of
\Omega
-valued sets (cf.
[
6
]
and
[
21
]
).

Let
X_{0}
be a set.
A
\mathfrak{Q}
-preorder
\varepsilon\colon{{X_{0}\times X_{0}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathfrak{Q}}
is a
\mathfrak{Q}
-valued equivalence relation
on
X_{0}
if
\varepsilon
satisfies the additional symmetry axiom:

(Symmetry)

\varepsilon({x},{y})=(\varepsilon({y},{x}))^{\prime},\qquad{x},{y}\in X_{0}

If
\varepsilon
is symmetric, then the pair
(X_{0},\varepsilon)
is called a
\mathfrak{Q}
-valued set
.

To identify a
\mathfrak{Q}
-valued set
(X_{0},\varepsilon)
with a symmetric
\mathfrak{Q}
-category, we define a type function on
X_{0}
as follows. Since (
7.2
) and the symmetry axiom imply that
\varepsilon(x,x)
is self-divisible and hermitian for each
x\in X_{0}
, we set:

(7.6)

t(x)=\varepsilon(x,x),\qquad x\in X_{0}.

This defines the typed set
X=(X_{0},t)
, and the
\mathfrak{Q}
-valued set
(X_{0},\varepsilon)
becomes a symmetric
D\mathfrak{Q}
-category
(X,\alpha)
,
where
\alpha
is given by
\alpha(x,y)={\varepsilon(y,y)}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 6.06888pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\textstyle{\varepsilon(x,y)}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 39.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 39.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces{\varepsilon(x,x)}
for all
{x,y\in X_{0}}
.
In this sense we always identify
\mathfrak{Q}
-valued sets with symmetric
D\mathfrak{Q}
-categories, where the type function is determined by (
7.6
).

Finally, note that the value

t(x)=\varepsilon(x,x)
of the type function at
x
,
interpreted as the
extent of existence
of
x
, provides a nontrivial semantic layer to
\mathfrak{Q}
-valued sets.
It ensures that the structure not only encodes relationships between elements but also reflects their individual degrees of presence or certainty within the system.

The category of
\mathfrak{Q}
-valued sets, with left adjoint distributors as morphisms, is denoted by
\mathtt{Set}(\mathfrak{Q})
. With regard to the results in Section
5
and Section
6
the following important fact holds.

Theorem 7.1
.

If the Cauchy completion w.r.t.
D\mathfrak{Q}
preserves the symmetry axiom, then
\mathtt{Set}(\mathfrak{Q})
is an
(
epi, extremal mono
)
-category.

Example 7.2
.

Let
(D\mathfrak{Q}_{0},\omega)
be defined by

\omega(a,b)=\mathop{\textstyle\bigvee}\{\,\lambda\in\mathfrak{Q}\mid{b}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.08333pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.47223pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\textstyle{\lambda}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 15.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 15.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces{a}\in\text{\rm hom}(b,a)\,\},\qquad a,b\in D\mathfrak{Q}_{0}.

We show that this pair forms a
\mathfrak{Q}
-valued set. First, we notice that
{b}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 6.98785pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\textstyle{\omega(a,b)}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 41.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 41.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces{a}
is the top element of the
hom
-space
\text{hom}(b,a)
— i.e.,
\tau(a,b)={b}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 6.98785pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\textstyle{\omega(a,b)}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 41.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 41.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces{a}
. Hence
(D\mathfrak{Q}_{0},\omega)
is the
\mathfrak{Q}
-valued preordered set induced by the
D\mathfrak{Q}
-category
(D\mathfrak{Q},\tau)
(cf. Example
2.2
).
Moreover
\omega
is symmetric — i.e.,
(D\mathfrak{Q}_{0},\omega)
is a
\mathfrak{Q}
-valued set. Referring to the divisibility condition (
7.2
) the following special property of
\omega
follows immediately from its definition:

(7.7)

\omega(a,b)=\omega(a,\omega(b,b))=\omega(\omega(a,a),b)=\omega(\omega(a,a),\omega(b,b)),\qquad a,b\in D\mathfrak{Q}_{0}.

Hence the associated hom-arrow-assignment
\xi
of the symmetric
D\mathfrak{Q}
-category
(D\mathfrak{Q},\xi)
corresponding to
(D\mathfrak{Q}_{0},\omega)
has the form:

\xi(a,b)={\omega(b,b)}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 7.48785pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\textstyle{\omega(a,b)}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 42.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 42.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces{\omega(a,a)}=\tau(\omega(a,a),\omega(b,b)),\qquad a,b\in D\mathfrak{Q}_{0},

where we have used (
7.7
). Since the relations (
7.1
) and (
7.2
) imply for all
a,b,c\in D\mathfrak{Q}_{0}

\displaystyle\omega(a,b)\ast(\omega(b,b)\searrow\omega(b,c))

\displaystyle=(\omega(a,b)\swarrow b)\ast\omega(b,b)\ast(\omega(b,b)\searrow\omega(b,c))

\displaystyle=\bigl(\omega(a,b)\swarrow b\bigr)\ast\omega(b,c),

the following property holds:

(7.8)

\tau(a,b)\circ_{b}\tau(b,c)={\omega(b,b)}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 7.48785pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\textstyle{\omega(a,b)}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 42.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 42.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces{a}\circ_{\omega(b,b)}{c}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 7.96703pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\textstyle{\omega(b,c)}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 42.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 42.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces{\omega(b,b)},\qquad a,b,c\in D\mathfrak{Q}_{0}.

Lemma 7.3
.

The
\mathfrak{Q}
-valued set
(D\mathfrak{Q}_{0},\omega)
is the terminal object in
\mathtt{Set}(\mathfrak{Q})
.

Proof.

Let
(X_{0},\varepsilon)
be a
\mathfrak{Q}
-valued set, which we identify with the symmetric
D\mathfrak{Q}
-category
(X,\alpha)
. Referring to (
7.7
) we define a map
\Phi\colon D\mathfrak{Q}_{0}\times X_{0}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\text{\rm morph}(D\mathfrak{Q})
by

\Phi(a,x)={\varepsilon(x,x)}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 11.47572pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\textstyle{\omega(a,\varepsilon(x,x))}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 74.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 74.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces{\omega(a,a)}=\tau(\omega(a,a),\varepsilon(x,x)),\qquad a\in D\mathfrak{Q}_{0},\,x\in X_{0}.

The relation
\tau(\omega(b,b),\omega(a,a))\mathbin{\circ_{\omega(a,a)}}\Phi(a,x)\leq\Phi(b,x)
is obvious. Further we observe for all
a,b\in D\mathfrak{Q}_{0}
and all
x,y\in X_{0}
:

\displaystyle\Phi(a,x)\mathbin{\circ_{\varepsilon(x,x)}}\alpha(x,y)\leq\Phi(a,x)\mathbin{\circ_{\varepsilon(x,x)}}\tau(\varepsilon(x,x),\varepsilon(y,y))\leq\Phi(a,y),

i.e.,
\Phi:(X,\alpha)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.06456pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.62848pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\circ}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 13.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 13.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces(D\mathfrak{Q},\xi)
is a distributor.

To show that
\Phi
is left adjoint, we define a distributor
\Psi\colon(D\mathfrak{Q},\xi)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.06456pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.62848pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\circ}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 13.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 13.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces(X,\alpha)
by

\Psi(x,a)={\omega(a,a)}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 8.97572pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\textstyle{\omega(\varepsilon(x,x),a)}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 69.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 69.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces{\varepsilon(x,x)}=\tau(\varepsilon(x,x),\omega(a,a)),\qquad x\in X_{0},\,a\in D\mathfrak{Q}_{0}.

Clearly,
\Phi(a,x)\mathbin{\circ_{\varepsilon(x,x)}}\Psi(x,b)\leq\tau(\omega(a,a),\omega(b,b))
holds. Further, we apply (
7.8
) and obtain:

\displaystyle\alpha(x,x)\leq\mathop{\textstyle\bigvee}_{a\in D\mathfrak{Q}_{0}}\tau(\varepsilon(x,x),a)\mathbin{\circ_{a}}\tau(a,\varepsilon(x,x))=\mathop{\textstyle\bigvee}_{a\in D\mathfrak{Q}_{0}}\bigl(\Psi(x,a)\mathbin{\circ_{\omega(a,a)}}\Phi(a,x)\bigr).

Hence
\Psi
is right adjoint to
\Phi
.

Let
\Phi^{\prime}\colon(X,\alpha)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.06456pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.62848pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\circ}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 13.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 13.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces(D\mathfrak{Q}_{0},\xi)
be an arbitrary left adjoint distributor with the corresponding right adjoint distributor
\Psi^{\prime}
. Since
\Phi^{\prime}(a,x)\in\text{hom}(\varepsilon(x,x),\omega(a,a))
, the relation
\Phi^{\prime}(a,x)\leq\Phi(a,x)
follows. Analogously, we verify
\Psi^{\prime}(x,a)\leq\Psi(x,a)
. By Proposition
2.3
, this implies that
\Phi
and
\Phi^{\prime}
coincide.
∎

Given that the type function of
\mathfrak{Q}
-valued sets is defined by equation (
7.6
), a
\mathfrak{Q}
-valued set
(X_{0},\alpha)
is
separated
if and only if the following implication holds for all
x,y\in X_{0}
:

\varepsilon(x,x)=\varepsilon(x,y)=\varepsilon(y,x)=\varepsilon(y,y)\implies x=y.

This condition ensures that distinct elements cannot be indistinguishable in terms of their mutual and self-relatedness, preserving the identity of elements in the enriched structure.

Proposition 7.4
.

Let
\mathfrak{Q}=(\mathfrak{Q},\ast,e,\mbox{}^{\prime})
be a unital and involutive quantale, and let
(D\mathfrak{Q}_{0},\omega)
be the terminal object in
\mathtt{Set}
(\mathfrak{Q})
. Then the following properties are equivalent
:

(1)

\mathfrak{Q}
is integral
(
i.e.,
e=\top
)
.

(2)

(D\mathfrak{Q}_{0},\omega)
is Cauchy complete.

(3)

(D\mathfrak{Q}_{0},\omega)
is separated.

Proof.

We begin by noting that both
\top
and
e

are hermitian elements of
\mathfrak{Q}
, that is,
{\top,e\in D\mathfrak{Q}_{0}}
.
Furthermore,
\text{\rm hom}(e,e)
is the quantale
\mathfrak{Q}
,
\text{\rm hom}(\top,\top)
is the
hom
-space of two-sided elements,

\text{\rm hom}(\top,e)
is the
hom
-space of right-sided elements, and
\text{\rm hom}(e,\top)
is the
hom
-space of left-sided elements of
\mathfrak{Q}
. From this, we conclude:

\omega(\top,\top)=\omega(\top,e)=\omega(e,\top)=\omega(e,e)=\top.

Consequently, if
(D\mathfrak{Q}_{0},\omega)
is separated, then we obtain
\top=e
— i.e.,
\mathfrak{Q}
is integral. Therefore, we have shown that
(3)
\Longrightarrow
(1). Since Cauchy completeness implies separation, it remains to prove (1)
\Longrightarrow
(2).

Let
\mu=(f,a,g)
be a presingleton of the symmetric
D\mathfrak{Q}
-category
(D\mathfrak{Q},\xi)
corresponding to
(D\mathfrak{Q}_{0},\omega)
.
Since
\mathfrak{Q}
is integral,
\omega(c,c)=c
follows for all
c\in D\mathfrak{Q}_{0}
— i.e. the type function coincides with the identity of
D\mathfrak{Q}_{0}
, and
consequently
{b}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 8.48785pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\textstyle{\omega(a,b)}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 44.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 44.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces{a}=\tau(a,b)
holds for all
a,b\in D\mathfrak{Q}_{0}
. Hence, we have
f(b)\leq\tau(a,b)
and
g(b)\leq\tau(b,a)
. By Proposition
2.3
, this implies
\mu=\widetilde{a}
— i.e.
\mu
is represented by the element
a
of
D\mathfrak{Q}_{0}
. Since the type function coincides with the identity, the uniqueness of
a
is evident.
∎

Comments 7.5
.

(1) Although the Cauchy completion w.r.t.
D\mathfrak{Q}
does not necessarily preserves the symmetry axiom, the proof of the implication (1)
\Longrightarrow
(2) in
Proposition
7.4
shows that in the case of integral and involutive
quantales
\mathfrak{Q}
, every presingleton of the terminal object in
\mathtt{Set}
(\mathfrak{Q})
is in fact a
singleton. This reflects a strong form of representability in the case of quantale-valued sets.

(2) If
\mathfrak{Q}
is non-integral and the Cauchy completion w.r.t.
D\mathfrak{Q}
preserves the symmetry axiom, then the Cauchy completion of
(D\mathfrak{Q}_{0},\omega)
is also the terminal object of
\mathtt{Set}(\mathfrak{Q})
(cf. Section
6
). Hence in this setting the category
\mathtt{Set}(\mathfrak{Q})
has always a Cauchy complete terminal object.

If
\mathfrak{Q}
is a frame
\Omega
with the identity map as involution, then
\mathtt{Set}(\Omega)
coincides with the category of
\Omega
-valued sets (cf.
[
3
, Sect. 2.8 and 2.9]
). Recently, in the special case of commutative, unital, and divisible quantales
(\mathfrak{Q},\ast,e)
, X. Hu and L. Shen showed that
\mathtt{Set}(\mathfrak{Q})
is a topos if and only if
\mathfrak{Q}
is a frame — i.e.,
\ast=\wedge
(cf.
[
16
]
). This result can be seen as a bridge between a special class of quatale-valued sets and topos theory, highlighting the foundational role of frames in categorical semantics.
